---
name: advanced-mip-patterns
description: Use when a task involves fixed charges, logical implications, min/max choices, sequencing, no-overlap, piecewise costs, routing-state logic, or other advanced MIP modeling patterns.
metadata:
  short-description: Advanced MIP formulation patterns
---

# Advanced MIP Patterns

## Use when

- The formulation needs logical implications, fixed charges, activation decisions, ordering, no-overlap, disjunctions, min/max, absolute values, tiers, route state, or resource calendars.
- A simple LP/MIP is producing unrealistic behavior because decisions are not linked tightly enough.
- The model is slow or infeasible and the likely cause is weak big-M logic, excessive pairwise variables, or missing state transitions.

## Do not use when

- A continuous LP is sufficient and adding binaries would not represent a real decision.
- The task is better handled by a specialized CP-SAT, constraint programming, nonlinear, or simulation method and the user did not require MIP.
- The user only needs a high-level explanation; do not introduce advanced constructs unless they clarify the actual model.

## General boundary rules

- Add binaries only for real yes/no choices, disjunctions, or nonconvex structure.
- Prefer the tightest formulation that matches the business rule; avoid decorative binaries.
- Use solver-native constructs when they improve clarity and are supported by the target environment.
- If a construct makes the model much slower, compare it with a simpler valid alternative before keeping it.

## Fixed charge and activation

- Use a binary `y` for whether an activity/resource is active.
- Link activity with a tight bound: `x <= U * y`.
- Add lower activation only when the business requires minimum use: `x >= L * y`.
- Charge fixed cost once through `fixed_cost * y`, not per unit.
- Do not force `y = 1` whenever `x = 0` unless the activity is mandatory.
- If there are startup/shutdown costs over time, separate active state from transition state.

## Logical rules

- Prefer solver-native indicator constraints for simple if-then rules when they keep the model clearer.
- If using big-M, derive M from data bounds and keep one M per rule family when possible.
- For either-or choices, use binaries whose sum is exactly one or at most one, matching the business rule.
- Distinguish implication (`A -> B`) from equivalence (`A <-> B`); the reverse direction often needs a separate constraint.
- For conditional capacity, link both activity and resource use to the condition.

## Min, max, absolute value

- For `z = max(a, b)`, add `z >= a`, `z >= b`, and objective pressure if minimizing z. Use binaries only when equality is needed without objective pressure.
- For absolute deviation `d = |x - target|`, add `d >= x - target` and `d >= target - x`.
- For service-level penalties, separate hard feasibility from soft penalty variables.
- For `min`, either transform with signs or add binaries when equality is not enforced by the objective.
- For ratio constraints, cross-multiply only when denominators are constant or safely bounded away from zero.

## Sequencing and no-overlap

- For jobs sharing a resource, use order binary `before[i,j]`.
- Typical pattern:
  - `start[j] >= end[i] - M * (1 - before[i,j])`
  - `start[i] >= end[j] - M * before[i,j]`
- Compute M from the scheduling horizon, not from an arbitrary large number.
- Include setup, cleanup, travel, rest, or turnover time in the correct side of the ordering constraint.
- Create pairwise order variables only for pairs that can share a resource or conflict.
- If time is discrete and the horizon is small, a time-indexed model may be cleaner than pairwise disjunctions.

## Piecewise and tiers

- Use solver-native piecewise-linear features for convex piecewise costs when suitable.
- For nonconvex tiers, use explicit segment variables and segment activation binaries.
- Ensure tier quantities sum to the original activity and each tier has its own upper bound.
- Distinguish incremental tiers from all-units tiers; their formulations and costs differ.
- Check whether discount tiers create nonconvex incentives that require binary selection.

## State and inventory logic

- State variables need initial conditions, transition equations, and terminal requirements if the business specifies them.
- Route or schedule state should be advanced in event order, not checked only row by row.
- Recovery, restock, cleaning, cooldown, maintenance, and rest rules often require cumulative state or last-event logic.
- If state can reset, model the reset trigger and its resource/time cost.

## Debugging advanced models

- First solve a relaxed or smaller instance and inspect whether the intended binaries activate.
- Add named constraints so IIS output points to business rules.
- If the reference solution is infeasible, replay the relevant state transitions manually before changing the evaluator.
- If the LP relaxation is very weak, inspect big-M values, missing lower/upper links, and symmetry.
- If the model is unexpectedly infeasible, test each advanced rule family on a tiny instance with a known feasible solution.

# Recycling MRF Stochastic Sorting Plan

A regional materials recovery facility must commit to a one-shift operating plan before the true quality of inbound recycling loads is known. The facility can open sorting lines, assign labor hours, and schedule quality-control tests before the shift. After each possible scenario is realized, it decides how many tons from each inbound batch to process on each eligible line and how many tons of each clean commodity to sell into buyer contracts.

You are planning one operating shift for a regional materials recovery facility (MRF). The facility receives municipal recycling batches from several collection zones. The same pre-shift staffing and line-opening plan must work across several possible realizations of inbound volume, material mix, labor drag, and buyer demand.

The planning goal is to maximize expected operating margin while protecting the facility against poor-quality storm-week collections and buyer shortfalls.


## Data

- `sorting_lines.csv`: sorting line capacities, technology family, accepted material streams, staffing limits, fixed and variable operating costs, and quality-control limits.
- `line_commodity_recovery.csv`: commodity recovery efficiency by line.
- `inbound_batches.csv`: inbound municipal batches, stream type, wet/high-contamination flags, available baseline tons, and any minimum processing commitment for priority municipal loads.
- `commodities.csv`: buyer commodity prices, contract floors, sales caps, and shortfall penalties.
- `scenarios.csv`: probabilities and scenario multipliers for inbound volume, demand, labor drag, contamination, and recovery.
- `scenario_batch_quality.csv`: scenario-specific available tons and commodity shares for each batch.
- `config.json`: facility-wide labor, quality-control, residue, unprocessed-material, and tail-risk settings.

## Operating Rules

The same line-opening, labor, and quality-control plan is used in all scenarios. Processing and sales decisions are scenario-specific.

Opened lines incur their fixed cost and must be assigned labor within the line's staffing range. Closed lines cannot receive labor, quality-control tests, or processing volume. The facility has a total labor-hour budget and a total quality-control test budget.

At least the required number of fiber, plastic, and container-capable lines must be opened so the plan does not depend on a single fragile technology family. Line families are listed in `sorting_lines.csv`; container-capable lines are those with family `metal`, `glass_metal`, or `mixed`.

A batch can only be processed on a line that accepts its stream. Wet batches cannot be processed on lines that disallow wet streams. High-contamination or mixed batches require enough quality-control tests on the line before they can be processed there.

In each scenario, a line's processed tons cannot exceed its scenario-adjusted capacity, and its labor use cannot exceed the labor hours assigned in the pre-shift plan. Each batch can be processed at most up to its scenario-specific available tons. Priority municipal batches with a positive `must_process_rate` must have at least that fraction processed in every scenario.

Recovered clean commodity tons depend on the processed batch, scenario quality shares, scenario recovery multiplier, and the line recovery table. Scenario sales for each commodity cannot exceed recovered clean tons or the scenario-adjusted contract cap. Contract floor shortfalls are allowed but penalized. Unprocessed material and processing residue also carry disposal or service penalties. A tail-risk penalty is charged on the worst priority-contract shortfall across scenarios.

## Submission Schema

Write `submissions/solution.csv` with exactly these columns:

```csv
row_type,line_id,scenario_id,batch_id,commodity_id,value
```

Use these row types:

- `open_line`: one row for every sorting line. `value` must be 0 or 1.
- `labor_hours`: one row for every sorting line. `value` is assigned labor hours.
- `qc_tests`: one row for every sorting line. `value` must be a nonnegative integer.
- `process`: scenario-specific tons from one batch processed on one line. Leave `commodity_id` blank.
- `sell`: scenario-specific clean commodity tons sold. Leave `line_id` and `batch_id` blank.

Processing quantities must use the 0.1-ton increment in `config.json`. Sales and staffing values may be decimal. You may omit zero-valued `process` and `sell` rows, but all first-stage rows are required.

---
name: optimization-modeling-core
description: Use for natural-language-to-optimization tasks that require translating a business problem into a complete mathematical model before writing solver code.
metadata:
  short-description: Turn business text into a full optimization model
---

# Optimization Modeling Core

## Use when

- The user gives a word problem, business process, paper scenario, or benchmark prompt and expects a formal optimization model.
- Solver code exists but the issue appears to be a missing or incorrect formulation.
- The task asks for a complete model, not only a heuristic, simulation, or data summary.

## Do not use when

- The task is purely about software plumbing, visualization, data cleaning, or report writing.
- The problem is already fully formulated and the user only asks for code translation into a specific solver; use the solver-specific skill as the primary guide.
- The request is exploratory and the user explicitly wants brainstorming without implementation.

## Model-first workflow

1. Restate the business decision in plain language.
2. Identify sets, indices, units, and time granularity.
3. Extract parameters from data files and confirm units.
4. Define decision variables with domains and business meaning.
5. Write the objective with every cost, reward, penalty, and service term.
6. Write constraints grouped by operational rule.
7. Check edge cases: missing eligibility, impossible demand, fixed commitments, and terminal-state requirements.
8. Only then implement code.

## Ambiguity handling

- If the brief is ambiguous but data and examples imply a convention, state the assumption and continue.
- If ambiguity changes feasibility, objective direction, or deliverable schema, ask before coding or implement the least risky interpretation with a clear note.
- If units conflict across files, normalize units explicitly before modeling.
- If a hard requirement conflicts with data, diagnose the conflict before adding slack variables.

## Formulation checklist

- Every row in an input table should either become a parameter, an eligibility rule, or be explicitly irrelevant.
- Every decision variable should appear in at least one binding constraint and usually in the objective.
- Demand balance should use the correct equality or inequality direction; do not weaken equality to `>=` unless surplus is genuinely allowed.
- Capacity constraints should include activation/open/setup binaries when the business says inactive resources cannot produce, ship, or serve.
- Shortage, backlog, overtime, outsourcing, and postponement variables need explicit cost and bounds.
- Multi-period models need state transition equations and initial/terminal states.
- If the user asks for explanation, describe the modeling logic in business terms after solving.

## Constraint boundary rules

- Hard constraints represent physical impossibility, policy mandates, legal requirements, or explicit benchmark feasibility rules.
- Soft constraints need penalty variables or objective terms; do not silently turn hard constraints into penalties.
- Service levels should specify numerator, denominator, eligible population, and time window.
- Fairness constraints should define the comparison group and whether unavailable resources count.
- Logical implications need both directions only when the business rule says equivalence, not merely implication.

## Objective boundary rules

- Confirm whether the problem minimizes cost, maximizes profit, maximizes service, or uses lexicographic priorities.
- Do not mix penalties with primary economics unless their scale is intentional.
- Include fixed costs only once per activated entity.
- Include revenue only for fulfilled demand or accepted jobs, not for unmet or canceled work.
- Keep reporting metrics separate from optimized objective terms unless the prompt explicitly optimizes them.

## Implementation expectations

- Use structured parsing for CSV/JSON instead of manual string manipulation.
- Keep solver code, solution export, and validation code clearly separated.
- For benchmark or task environments, read the provided brief before coding and follow the expected output schema exactly.
- When comparing two answers, diagnose concrete formulation differences and objective consequences, not only feasibility.
- Write exported solutions at the precision expected by the checker; avoid unnecessary rounding.
- Preserve the input schema and output schema unless the user asks to redesign the benchmark.

## Verification boundary

- Verify feasibility independently from solver status when an evaluator or checker exists.
- Recompute objective from exported artifacts, not only from in-memory solver expressions.
- For benchmark tasks, create at least one negative check for each newly added rule when practical.
- For paper or real-world tasks, separate what is directly supported by the source from synthetic or assumed data.

## Red flags

- Objective terms that are counted twice or charged at the wrong marginal rate.
- Binary variables not linked tightly enough to continuous activity.
- Time periods shifted by one index.
- Average or fairness constraints that ignore the denominator or eligibility set.
- Public prompt files that reveal solver-ready variables or formulas when the benchmark is meant to test modeling translation.
- A feasible solution that exploits missing eligibility, free disposal, negative cycles, or unbounded production.
- A model that reports optimality after a time limit, infeasible status, or missing incumbent.

    if r['row_type']=='process': proc[(r['line_id'],r['scenario_id'])]+=float(r['value'])

    worst=[]
    for line in labor:
        tons=proc[(line,sc)]
        dat=lines[line]
        req=float(scens[sc]['labor_multiplier'])*(float(dat['base_labor_hours'])+float(dat['labor_hours_per_ton'])*tons)
        slack=labor[line]-req
        if tons or labor[line]:
            worst.append((slack,line,tons,req,labor[line]))
    worst.sort()
    print(sc, 'min labor slack', worst[0])

/bin/bash -lc "python - <<'PY'
import csv
from collections import defaultdict
sol=list(csv.DictReader(open('/app/submissions/solution.csv', newline='')))
lines={r['line_id']:r for r in csv.DictReader(open('/app/data/sorting_lines.csv', newline=''))}
scens={r['scenario_id']:r for r in csv.DictReader(open('/app/data/scenarios.csv', newline=''))}
labor={r['line_id']:float(r['value']) for r in sol if r['row_type']=='labor_hours'}
proc=defaultdict(float)
for r in sol:
    if r['row_type']=='process': proc[(r['line_id'],r['scenario_id'])]+=float(r['value'])
for formula in ['base+mult*var','mult*(base+var)','base+var<=labor/mult']:
    minslack=(1e9,None)
    for sc in scens:
        for line in labor:
            tons=proc[(line,sc)]
            dat=lines[line]
            base=float(dat['base_labor_hours']); var=float(dat['labor_hours_per_ton'])
            mult=float(scens[sc]['labor_multiplier'])
            if formula=='base+mult*var': req=base+mult*var*tons
            elif formula=='mult*(base+var)': req=mult*(base+var*tons)
            else: req=base+var*tons - labor[line]/mult
            slack=labor[line]-req if formula"'!='"'base+var<=labor/mult' else -req
            if slack<minslack[0]: minslack=(slack,(sc,line,tons,req,labor[line],mult))
    print(formula,minslack)

            slack=labor[line]-req if formula"'!='"'base+var<=labor/mult' else -req

/bin/bash -lc "python - <<'PY'
import csv, math
from collections import defaultdict
sol=list(csv.DictReader(open('/app/submissions/solution.csv', newline='')))
lines={r['line_id']:r for r in csv.DictReader(open('/app/data/sorting_lines.csv', newline=''))}
scens={r['scenario_id']:r for r in csv.DictReader(open('/app/data/scenarios.csv', newline=''))}
proc=defaultdict(float)
for r in sol:
    if r['row_type']=='process': proc[(r['line_id'],r['scenario_id'])]+=float(r['value'])
max_over=(-1,None)
for (line,sc),tons in proc.items():
    cap=float(lines[line]['capacity_tons'])/float(scens[sc]['labor_multiplier'])
    over=tons-cap
    if over>max_over[0]: max_over=(over,(sc,line,tons,cap))
print('max over cap/labor_mult',max_over)
# show positives > 1e-5
for (line,sc),tons in sorted(proc.items(), key=lambda kv:(kv[0][1],kv[0][0])):
    cap=float(lines[line]['capacity_tons'])/float(scens[sc]['labor_multiplier'])
    over=tons-cap
    if over>1e-5:
        print('VIOL',sc,line,tons,cap,over)

/bin/bash -lc "python - <<'PY'
import csv
from collections import defaultdict
# load
sol=list(csv.DictReader(open('/app/submissions/solution.csv', newline='')))
lines={r['line_id']:r for r in csv.DictReader(open('/app/data/sorting_lines.csv', newline=''))}
scens={r['scenario_id']:r for r in csv.DictReader(open('/app/data/scenarios.csv', newline=''))}
qual={(r['scenario_id'],r['batch_id']):r for r in csv.DictReader(open('/app/data/scenario_batch_quality.csv', newline=''))}
rec={(r['line_id'],r['commodity_id']):float(r['recovery_efficiency']) for r in csv.DictReader(open('/app/data/line_commodity_recovery.csv', newline=''))}
comms={r['commodity_id']:r for r in csv.DictReader(open('/app/data/commodities.csv', newline=''))}
# compute recovered
recovered=defaultdict(float)
for r in sol:
    if r['row_type']"'!='"'process':
        continue
    sc=r['scenario_id']; b=r['batch_id']; l=r['line_id']; tons=float(r['value'])
    q=qual[(sc,b)]
    rm=float(scens[sc]['recovery_multiplier'])
    shares={'OCC':'occ_share','MIXPAPER':'mixed_paper_share','PET':'pet_share','HDPE':'hdpe_share','ALUM':'alum_share'}
    for c,col in shares.items():
        recovered[(sc,c)] += tons*float(q[col])*rm*rec[(l,c)]
# compare sells
viol=[]
for r in sol:
    if r['row_type']"'!='"'sell':
        continue
    sc=r['scenario_id']; c=r['commodity_id']; sell=float(r['value'])
    recov=recovered[(sc,c)]
    cap=float(comms[c]['contract_max_tons'])*float(scens[sc]['demand_multiplier'])
    if sell-recov>1e-5 or sell-cap>1e-5:
        viol.append((sc,c,sell,recov,cap))
print('sell violations', viol[:20], 'count', len(viol))
# print slack summary
for sc in sorted(scens):
    for c in comms:
        sells=[float(r['value']) for r in sol if r['row_type']=='sell' and r['scenario_id']==sc and r['commodity_id']==c]
        s=sells[0] if sells else 0.0
        recov=recovered[(sc,c)]
        cap=float(comms[c]['contract_max_tons'])*float(scens[sc]['demand_multiplier'])
        if abs(s-recov)<1e-4 or abs(s-cap)<1e-4:
            print('binding',sc,c,'sell',round(s,6),'recov',round(recov,6),'cap',round(cap,6))
PY"

import csv
from collections import defaultdict
sol=list(csv.DictReader(open('/app/submissions/solution.csv', newline='')))
qual={(r['scenario_id'],r['batch_id']):r for r in csv.DictReader(open('/app/data/scenario_batch_quality.csv', newline=''))}

    if r['row_type']=='process': proc[(r['scenario_id'],r['batch_id'])]+=float(r['value'])
viol=[]
for key,q in qual.items():
    sc,b=key
    avail=float(q['available_tons'])
    used=proc[key]
    if used-avail>1e-5:
        viol.append(('avail',sc,b,used,avail,used-avail))
    rate=float(batches[b]['must_process_rate'])
    if rate>0:
        req=rate*avail
        if req-used>1e-5:
            viol.append(('must',sc,b,used,req,req-used))
print('violations', len(viol))
for v in viol[:40]:
    print(v)

import csv, json
sol=list(csv.DictReader(open('/app/submissions/solution.csv', newline='')))
lines={r['line_id']:r for r in csv.DictReader(open('/app/data/sorting_lines.csv', newline=''))}
config=json.load(open('/app/data/config.json'))
openv={r['line_id']:float(r['value']) for r in sol if r['row_type']=='open_line'}
labor={r['line_id']:float(r['value']) for r in sol if r['row_type']=='labor_hours'}
qc={r['line_id']:float(r['value']) for r in sol if r['row_type']=='qc_tests'}
# family counts
container={'metal','glass_metal','mixed'}
res=[]
for line,row in lines.items():
    o=openv[line]; lh=labor[line]; q=qc[line]
    minlab=float(row['min_labor_hours']); maxlab=float(row['max_labor_hours'])
    minqc=float(row['min_qc_tests']); maxqc=float(row['max_qc_tests'])
    if o not in (0.0,1.0): res.append(('open binary', line, o))
    if abs(q-round(q))>1e-8 or q<0: res.append(('qc integer',line,q))
    if lh < minlab*o -1e-5 or lh > maxlab*o +1e-5: res.append(('labor bounds',line,lh,(minlab*o,maxlab*o)))
    if q < minqc*o -1e-5 or q > maxqc*o +1e-5: res.append(('qc bounds',line,q,(minqc*o,maxqc*o)))
print('first-stage violations',res)
print('total labor',sum(labor.values()),'budget',config['total_labor_hours_budget'])
print('total qc',sum(qc.values()),'budget',config['total_qc_tests'])
print('open families',sum(openv[l] for l,row in lines.items() if row['family']=='fiber'),sum(openv[l] for l,row in lines.items() if row['family']=='plastic'),sum(openv[l] for l,row in lines.items() if row['family'] in container))

---
name: optimization-modeling-core
description: Use for natural-language-to-optimization tasks that require translating a business problem into a complete mathematical model before writing solver code.
metadata:
  short-description: Turn business text into a full optimization model
---

# Optimization Modeling Core

## Use when

- The user gives a word problem, business process, paper scenario, or benchmark prompt and expects a formal optimization model.
- Solver code exists but the issue appears to be a missing or incorrect formulation.
- The task asks for a complete model, not only a heuristic, simulation, or data summary.

## Do not use when

- The task is purely about software plumbing, visualization, data cleaning, or report writing.
- The problem is already fully formulated and the user only asks for code translation into a specific solver; use the solver-specific skill as the primary guide.
- The request is exploratory and the user explicitly wants brainstorming without implementation.

## Model-first workflow

1. Restate the business decision in plain language.
2. Identify sets, indices, units, and time granularity.
3. Extract parameters from data files and confirm units.
4. Define decision variables with domains and business meaning.
5. Write the objective with every cost, reward, penalty, and service term.
6. Write constraints grouped by operational rule.
7. Check edge cases: missing eligibility, impossible demand, fixed commitments, and terminal-state requirements.
8. Only then implement code.

## Ambiguity handling

- If the brief is ambiguous but data and examples imply a convention, state the assumption and continue.
- If ambiguity changes feasibility, objective direction, or deliverable schema, ask before coding or implement the least risky interpretation with a clear note.
- If units conflict across files, normalize units explicitly before modeling.
- If a hard requirement conflicts with data, diagnose the conflict before adding slack variables.

## Formulation checklist

- Every row in an input table should either become a parameter, an eligibility rule, or be explicitly irrelevant.
- Every decision variable should appear in at least one binding constraint and usually in the objective.
- Demand balance should use the correct equality or inequality direction; do not weaken equality to `>=` unless surplus is genuinely allowed.
- Capacity constraints should include activation/open/setup binaries when the business says inactive resources cannot produce, ship, or serve.
- Shortage, backlog, overtime, outsourcing, and postponement variables need explicit cost and bounds.
- Multi-period models need state transition equations and initial/terminal states.
- If the user asks for explanation, describe the modeling logic in business terms after solving.

## Constraint boundary rules

- Hard constraints represent physical impossibility, policy mandates, legal requirements, or explicit benchmark feasibility rules.
- Soft constraints need penalty variables or objective terms; do not silently turn hard constraints into penalties.
- Service levels should specify numerator, denominator, eligible population, and time window.
- Fairness constraints should define the comparison group and whether unavailable resources count.
- Logical implications need both directions only when the business rule says equivalence, not merely implication.

## Objective boundary rules

- Confirm whether the problem minimizes cost, maximizes profit, maximizes service, or uses lexicographic priorities.
- Do not mix penalties with primary economics unless their scale is intentional.
- Include fixed costs only once per activated entity.
- Include revenue only for fulfilled demand or accepted jobs, not for unmet or canceled work.
- Keep reporting metrics separate from optimized objective terms unless the prompt explicitly optimizes them.

## Implementation expectations

- Use structured parsing for CSV/JSON instead of manual string manipulation.
- Keep solver code, solution export, and validation code clearly separated.
- For benchmark or task environments, read the provided brief before coding and follow the expected output schema exactly.
- When comparing two answers, diagnose concrete formulation differences and objective consequences, not only feasibility.
- Write exported solutions at the precision expected by the checker; avoid unnecessary rounding.
- Preserve the input schema and output schema unless the user asks to redesign the benchmark.

## Verification boundary

- Verify feasibility independently from solver status when an evaluator or checker exists.
- Recompute objective from exported artifacts, not only from in-memory solver expressions.
- For benchmark tasks, create at least one negative check for each newly added rule when practical.
- For paper or real-world tasks, separate what is directly supported by the source from synthetic or assumed data.

## Red flags

- Objective terms that are counted twice or charged at the wrong marginal rate.
- Binary variables not linked tightly enough to continuous activity.
- Time periods shifted by one index.
- Average or fairness constraints that ignore the denominator or eligibility set.
- Public prompt files that reveal solver-ready variables or formulas when the benchmark is meant to test modeling translation.
- A feasible solution that exploits missing eligibility, free disposal, negative cycles, or unbounded production.
- A model that reports optimality after a time limit, infeasible status, or missing incumbent.

---
name: or-pyscipopt
description: Use when building, running, validating, or debugging Python optimization models that use PySCIPOpt and SCIP.
metadata:
  short-description: Build and run PySCIPOpt models
---

# OR PySCIPOpt

## Use when

- The task asks for a Python optimization model using SCIP or `pyscipopt`.
- Existing solver code imports `pyscipopt`, sets SCIP parameters, writes `.lp`/`.mps`, or calls a SCIP-based checker.
- The user asks to run, debug, speed up, validate, or explain a SCIP-based solve.

## Do not use when

- The task only needs a mathematical formulation and no solver-specific code.
- The project clearly uses a different solver stack, such as Pyomo with CBC/HiGHS, OR-Tools CP-SAT, PuLP, CPLEX, or cvxpy, unless the user asks to port it to PySCIPOpt.
- The model is nonlinear or stochastic in a way that SCIP cannot handle directly; first decide whether it can be linearized or approximated.

## Environment rule

Use the Python environment specified by the user or the project. In this ORClaw workspace, the default solver environment is the `or` conda environment with PySCIPOpt available:

```bash
conda run -n or python <script.py>
```

If `pyscipopt` is missing, report the missing dependency, identify the command that failed, and try the project-specified environment before changing solver stacks.

## Workflow

1. Read the task brief and input files before coding.
2. Write the full mathematical model first: sets, parameters, decision variables, objective, and constraints.
3. Implement the model with explicit tuple keys and named constraints.
4. Solve with the selected Python command.
5. Export the solution using the exact schema required by the task.
6. Validate with the task checker or an independent recomputation of feasibility and objective.
7. If the solution is not accepted, diagnose whether the issue is formulation, data parsing, export formatting, tolerance, or evaluator mismatch before changing the objective or constraints.

## PySCIPOpt patterns

- Use `from pyscipopt import Model, quicksum`.
- Create sparse variable dictionaries explicitly, for example:

```python
x = {(i, j, t): model.addVar(vtype="B", name=f"x[{i},{j},{t}]") for i, j, t in keys}
```

- Use `quicksum(...)` for large linear expressions.
- Use `model.addCons(expr <= rhs, name="capacity[k,t]")` for constraints.
- Use `model.setObjective(expr, "minimize")` or `model.setObjective(expr, "maximize")`.
- Set reproducibility and limits with SCIP parameters:

```python
model.setParam("limits/gap", 0.0005)
model.setParam("limits/time", time_limit)
model.setParam("display/verblevel", 0)
```

- After `model.optimize()`, inspect `model.getStatus()` and only read values if a solution exists.
- Read variable values with `model.getVal(x[key])`, not `x[key].X`.
- Read objective and bound with `model.getObjVal()` and `model.getDualbound()`.
- Read relative gap with `model.getGap()` when available.

## Modeling hygiene

- Calibrate every big-M from data. If a tight M is not obvious, derive a bound and document it in a short comment.
- Keep feasibility checks, objective recomputation, and reference-optimality comparison separate.
- Do not round exported decision values unless the schema explicitly requires it.
- For benchmark work, treat the evaluator or checker as the scoring authority and the reference solver as a validation baseline.
- Keep solver logs or a concise solve summary when the user needs auditability.
- Avoid changing data to make a model feasible unless the task is explicitly to design or repair an instance.

## Debugging boundaries

- If a model is infeasible, inspect hard lower bounds, conservation equations, and mutually exclusive binary logic before relaxing constraints.
- If objective values differ, recompute each objective component from exported decisions.
- If runtime is high, first remove dominated variables, tighten big-M values, add eligibility filters, and check whether binaries are really necessary.
- If SCIP returns an incumbent with a gap, say so clearly; do not call it exact optimality unless the status and gap support that claim.
- If an evaluator rejects a solution that the model considers feasible, replay the rejected rule directly from the exported file before changing solver code.

## Useful commands

```bash
python -c "import pyscipopt; print(pyscipopt.__version__)"
conda run -n or python -c "import pyscipopt; print(pyscipopt.__version__)"
<python-command> <solver-script.py>
<python-command> <checker-script.py> --solution <solution-file> --env-dir <task-dir>
```

# Advanced MIP Patterns

## Use when

- The formulation needs logical implications, fixed charges, activation decisions, ordering, no-overlap, disjunctions, min/max, absolute values, tiers, route state, or resource calendars.
- A simple LP/MIP is producing unrealistic behavior because decisions are not linked tightly enough.
- The model is slow or infeasible and the likely cause is weak big-M logic, excessive pairwise variables, or missing state transitions.

## Do not use when

- A continuous LP is sufficient and adding binaries would not represent a real decision.
- The task is better handled by a specialized CP-SAT, constraint programming, nonlinear, or simulation method and the user did not require MIP.
- The user only needs a high-level explanation; do not introduce advanced constructs unless they clarify the actual model.

## General boundary rules

- Add binaries only for real yes/no choices, disjunctions, or nonconvex structure.
- Prefer the tightest formulation that matches the business rule; avoid decorative binaries.
- Use solver-native constructs when they improve clarity and are supported by the target environment.
- If a construct makes the model much slower, compare it with a simpler valid alternative before keeping it.

## Fixed charge and activation

- Use a binary `y` for whether an activity/resource is active.
- Link activity with a tight bound: `x <= U * y`.
- Add lower activation only when the business requires minimum use: `x >= L * y`.
- Charge fixed cost once through `fixed_cost * y`, not per unit.
- Do not force `y = 1` whenever `x = 0` unless the activity is mandatory.
- If there are startup/shutdown costs over time, separate active state from transition state.

## Logical rules

- Prefer solver-native indicator constraints for simple if-then rules when they keep the model clearer.
- If using big-M, derive M from data bounds and keep one M per rule family when possible.
- For either-or choices, use binaries whose sum is exactly one or at most one, matching the business rule.
- Distinguish implication (`A -> B`) from equivalence (`A <-> B`); the reverse direction often needs a separate constraint.
- For conditional capacity, link both activity and resource use to the condition.

## Min, max, absolute value

- For `z = max(a, b)`, add `z >= a`, `z >= b`, and objective pressure if minimizing z. Use binaries only when equality is needed without objective pressure.
- For absolute deviation `d = |x - target|`, add `d >= x - target` and `d >= target - x`.
- For service-level penalties, separate hard feasibility from soft penalty variables.
- For `min`, either transform with signs or add binaries when equality is not enforced by the objective.
- For ratio constraints, cross-multiply only when denominators are constant or safely bounded away from zero.

## Sequencing and no-overlap

- For jobs sharing a resource, use order binary `before[i,j]`.
- Typical pattern:
  - `start[j] >= end[i] - M * (1 - before[i,j])`
  - `start[i] >= end[j] - M * before[i,j]`
- Compute M from the scheduling horizon, not from an arbitrary large number.
- Include setup, cleanup, travel, rest, or turnover time in the correct side of the ordering constraint.
- Create pairwise order variables only for pairs that can share a resource or conflict.
- If time is discrete and the horizon is small, a time-indexed model may be cleaner than pairwise disjunctions.

## Piecewise and tiers

- Use solver-native piecewise-linear features for convex piecewise costs when suitable.
- For nonconvex tiers, use explicit segment variables and segment activation binaries.
- Ensure tier quantities sum to the original activity and each tier has its own upper bound.
- Distinguish incremental tiers from all-units tiers; their formulations and costs differ.
- Check whether discount tiers create nonconvex incentives that require binary selection.

## State and inventory logic

- State variables need initial conditions, transition equations, and terminal requirements if the business specifies them.
- Route or schedule state should be advanced in event order, not checked only row by row.
- Recovery, restock, cleaning, cooldown, maintenance, and rest rules often require cumulative state or last-event logic.
- If state can reset, model the reset trigger and its resource/time cost.

## Debugging advanced models

- First solve a relaxed or smaller instance and inspect whether the intended binaries activate.
- Add named constraints so IIS output points to business rules.
- If the reference solution is infeasible, replay the relevant state transitions manually before changing the evaluator.
- If the LP relaxation is very weak, inspect big-M values, missing lower/upper links, and symmetry.
- If the model is unexpectedly infeasible, test each advanced rule family on a tiny instance with a known feasible solution.

# Recycling MRF Stochastic Sorting Plan

A regional materials recovery facility must commit to a one-shift operating plan before the true quality of inbound recycling loads is known. The facility can open sorting lines, assign labor hours, and schedule quality-control tests before the shift. After each possible scenario is realized, it decides how many tons from each inbound batch to process on each eligible line and how many tons of each clean commodity to sell into buyer contracts.

You are planning one operating shift for a regional materials recovery facility (MRF). The facility receives municipal recycling batches from several collection zones. The same pre-shift staffing and line-opening plan must work across several possible realizations of inbound volume, material mix, labor drag, and buyer demand.

The planning goal is to maximize expected operating margin while protecting the facility against poor-quality storm-week collections and buyer shortfalls.


## Data

- `sorting_lines.csv`: sorting line capacities, technology family, accepted material streams, staffing limits, fixed and variable operating costs, and quality-control limits.
- `line_commodity_recovery.csv`: commodity recovery efficiency by line.
- `inbound_batches.csv`: inbound municipal batches, stream type, wet/high-contamination flags, available baseline tons, and any minimum processing commitment for priority municipal loads.
- `commodities.csv`: buyer commodity prices, contract floors, sales caps, and shortfall penalties.
- `scenarios.csv`: probabilities and scenario multipliers for inbound volume, demand, labor drag, contamination, and recovery.
- `scenario_batch_quality.csv`: scenario-specific available tons and commodity shares for each batch.
- `config.json`: facility-wide labor, quality-control, residue, unprocessed-material, and tail-risk settings.

## Operating Rules

The same line-opening, labor, and quality-control plan is used in all scenarios. Processing and sales decisions are scenario-specific.

Opened lines incur their fixed cost and must be assigned labor within the line's staffing range. Closed lines cannot receive labor, quality-control tests, or processing volume. The facility has a total labor-hour budget and a total quality-control test budget.

At least the required number of fiber, plastic, and container-capable lines must be opened so the plan does not depend on a single fragile technology family. Line families are listed in `sorting_lines.csv`; container-capable lines are those with family `metal`, `glass_metal`, or `mixed`.

A batch can only be processed on a line that accepts its stream. Wet batches cannot be processed on lines that disallow wet streams. High-contamination or mixed batches require enough quality-control tests on the line before they can be processed there.

In each scenario, a line's processed tons cannot exceed its scenario-adjusted capacity, and its labor use cannot exceed the labor hours assigned in the pre-shift plan. Each batch can be processed at most up to its scenario-specific available tons. Priority municipal batches with a positive `must_process_rate` must have at least that fraction processed in every scenario.

Recovered clean commodity tons depend on the processed batch, scenario quality shares, scenario recovery multiplier, and the line recovery table. Scenario sales for each commodity cannot exceed recovered clean tons or the scenario-adjusted contract cap. Contract floor shortfalls are allowed but penalized. Unprocessed material and processing residue also carry disposal or service penalties. A tail-risk penalty is charged on the worst priority-contract shortfall across scenarios.

## Submission Schema

Write `submissions/solution.csv` with exactly these columns:

```csv
row_type,line_id,scenario_id,batch_id,commodity_id,value
```

Use these row types:

- `open_line`: one row for every sorting line. `value` must be 0 or 1.
- `labor_hours`: one row for every sorting line. `value` is assigned labor hours.
- `qc_tests`: one row for every sorting line. `value` must be a nonnegative integer.
- `process`: scenario-specific tons from one batch processed on one line. Leave `commodity_id` blank.
- `sell`: scenario-specific clean commodity tons sold. Leave `line_id` and `batch_id` blank.

Processing quantities must use the 0.1-ton increment in `config.json`. Sales and staffing values may be decimal. You may omit zero-valued `process` and `sell` rows, but all first-stage rows are required.

I have enough context to lock the structure. This is best treated as a two-stage stochastic mixed-integer assignment/flow model, not routing or time-indexed scheduling: first-stage line opening, labor, and QC decisions are shared across scenarios; second-stage recourse is scenario-specific batch-to-line processing and commodity sales.

Planned model:
1. First stage: binary `open_line`, continuous `labor_hours`, integer `qc_tests`.
2. Second stage by scenario: integer `process_units` on eligible batch-line arcs using 0.1-ton units, continuous `sell`, continuous `shortfall`, and a worst-case priority-shortfall variable for the tail penalty.
3. Core linking: family-opening rules, labor/QC budgets, line staffing/QC bounds, scenario capacity/labor limits, batch availability and must-process commitments, recovery-based sales limits, contract shortfall penalties, unprocessed/residue penalties, and nonanticipativity through shared first-stage variables.
4. Extra formulation detail: risky mixed/high-contamination processing gets binary activation variables so QC thresholds are enforced tightly without nonlinear terms.

result56. Command output
# Recycling MRF Stochastic Sorting Plan

## Modeling choice

This instance is best modeled as a **two-stage stochastic mixed-integer assignment/flow problem**.

- First-stage decisions are shared across all scenarios:
  - line opening
  - assigned labor hours
  - assigned quality-control tests
- Second-stage recourse decisions are scenario-specific:
  - batch-to-line processing quantities
  - commodity sales

Because processing must respect a `0.1` ton increment, processing is modeled in integer units of size `delta = 0.1` ton. Sales and labor remain continuous. The deterministic equivalent is small enough to solve directly in SCIP.

## Sets and indices

- `L`: sorting lines, indexed by `l`
- `B`: inbound batches, indexed by `b`
- `S`: scenarios, indexed by `s`
- `C`: commodities, indexed by `c`
- `E subseteq B x L`: eligible batch-line pairs
  - `stream(b)` must be accepted by line `l`
  - if `wet_stream(b)=1`, then `wet_stream_allowed(l)=1`
- `R subseteq E`: risky eligible pairs, where batch `b` is risky
  - `stream(b)="mixed"` or `high_contamination_risk(b)=1`
- `F_fiber subseteq L`: lines with family `fiber`
- `F_plastic subseteq L`: lines with family `plastic`
- `F_container subseteq L`: lines with family `metal`, `glass_metal`, or `mixed`
- `C_pri subseteq C`: commodities with `priority_contract=1`

## Parameters

All numeric parameters come from the public data files.

- `p_s`: probability of scenario `s`
- `cap_l`: nominal tons capacity of line `l`
- `minopen_l`: minimum tons processed on line `l` if opened
- `fix_l`: fixed opening cost of line `l`
- `vcost_l`: variable processing cost per ton on line `l`
- `base_lab_l`: base labor hours for line `l`
- `labpt_l`: labor hours per processed ton on line `l`
- `labmin_l, labmax_l`: labor-hour bounds on line `l`
- `qcmin_l, qcmax_l`: QC-test bounds on line `l`
- `avail_sb`: scenario-available tons of batch `b`
- `share_sbc`: commodity share of commodity `c` in batch `b` under scenario `s`
- `rec_lc`: recovery efficiency of commodity `c` on line `l`
- `recmult_s`: scenario recovery multiplier
- `labmult_s`: scenario labor-drag multiplier
- `demmult_s`: scenario demand multiplier
- `contmult_s`: scenario contamination multiplier
- `price_c`: sale price per ton of commodity `c`
- `floor_c`: contract floor tons of commodity `c`
- `capdem_c`: contract maximum tons of commodity `c`
- `shortpen_c`: shortfall penalty per ton of commodity `c`
- `mpr_b`: must-process rate of batch `b`
- `priority_b`: municipal-priority flag of batch `b`
- `pen_unproc_b`: unprocessed penalty per ton of batch `b`
  - `unprocessed_priority_penalty_per_ton` if `priority_b=1`
  - `unprocessed_regular_penalty_per_ton` otherwise
- `labor_cost`: labor cost per hour
- `qc_cost`: cost per QC test
- `residue_cost`: residue disposal cost per ton
- `tail_pen`: tail penalty on worst priority-contract shortfall
- `H`: total labor-hour budget
- `Q`: total QC-test budget
- `reqfiber, reqplastic, reqcontainer`: required line counts by family group
- `delta = 0.1`: processing ton increment

Derived parameters:

- `Ucap_sl = floor((cap_l / labmult_s) / delta)`: scenario-adjusted line-capacity upper bound in processing units
- `Uavail_sb = avail_sb / delta`: batch availability in processing units
- `Lmust_sb = ceil(mpr_b * Uavail_sb)`: minimum required processed units for batch `b` in scenario `s`
- `Lopen_l = ceil(minopen_l / delta)`: minimum processed units on an open line
- `alpha_sblc = delta * share_sbc * recmult_s * rec_lc`: clean tons of commodity `c` recovered from one processing unit of `(s,b,l)`
- `beta_sbl = sum_{c in C} alpha_sblc`: total clean recovered tons from one processing unit of `(s,b,l)`
- `qcreq_sl = ceil(qcmin_l * contmult_s)`: QC tests required on line `l` in scenario `s` for risky processing

## Decision variables

First stage:

- `y_l in {0,1}`: `1` if line `l` is opened
- `h_l >= 0`: labor hours assigned to line `l`
- `q_l in Z_+`: QC tests assigned to line `l`

Second stage:

- `x_sbl in Z_+`: processing quantity on eligible pair `(b,l)` in scenario `s`, in units of `delta`
- `u_sbl in {0,1}` for `(b,l) in R`: `1` if risky batch `b` is processed on line `l` in scenario `s`
- `sell_sc >= 0`: clean tons of commodity `c` sold in scenario `s`
- `short_sc >= 0`: contract-floor shortfall for commodity `c` in scenario `s`
- `tail >= 0`: worst aggregate priority-contract shortfall across scenarios

## Objective

Maximize expected operating margin:

```text
max
  sum_{s in S} p_s [
      sum_{c in C} price_c * sell_sc
    - sum_{(b,l) in E} vcost_l * delta * x_sbl
    - sum_{c in C} shortpen_c * short_sc
    - sum_{b in B} pen_unproc_b * (avail_sb - delta * sum_{l:(b,l) in E} x_sbl)
    - residue_cost * (delta * sum_{(b,l) in E} x_sbl - sum_{(b,l) in E} beta_sbl * x_sbl)
  ]
  - sum_{l in L} fix_l * y_l
  - sum_{l in L} labor_cost * h_l
  - sum_{l in L} qc_cost * q_l
  - tail_pen * tail
```

## Constraints

### 1. First-stage operating-plan constraints

Line-family coverage:

```text
sum_{l in F_fiber} y_l >= reqfiber
sum_{l in F_plastic} y_l >= reqplastic
sum_{l in F_container} y_l >= reqcontainer
```

Line staffing and QC bounds:

```text
labmin_l * y_l <= h_l <= labmax_l * y_l          for all l in L
qcmin_l  * y_l <= q_l <= qcmax_l  * y_l          for all l in L
```

Facility budgets:

```text
sum_{l in L} h_l <= H
sum_{l in L} q_l <= Q
```

### 2. Scenario line-operation constraints

Scenario-adjusted line capacity:

```text
sum_{b:(b,l) in E} x_sbl <= Ucap_sl * y_l        for all s in S, l in L
```

Minimum tons on an opened line:

```text
sum_{b:(b,l) in E} x_sbl >= Lopen_l * y_l        for all s in S, l in L
```

Labor sufficiency for each scenario:

```text
base_lab_l * y_l + labpt_l * delta * sum_{b:(b,l) in E} x_sbl <= h_l
                                                 for all s in S, l in L
```

### 3. Batch feasibility and commitments

Availability:

```text
sum_{l:(b,l) in E} x_sbl <= Uavail_sb            for all s in S, b in B
```

Must-process commitments:

```text
sum_{l:(b,l) in E} x_sbl >= Lmust_sb             for all s in S, b in B with mpr_b > 0
```

### 4. QC logic for risky processing

Risky-use activation:

```text
x_sbl <= U_sbl * u_sbl                           for all s in S, (b,l) in R
```

QC requirement for risky processing:

```text
q_l >= qcreq_sl * u_sbl                          for all s in S, (b,l) in R
```

where `U_sbl` is the processing-unit upper bound for that arc.

### 5. Recovery and sales constraints

Recovered clean tons by commodity:

```text
sell_sc <= sum_{(b,l) in E} alpha_sblc * x_sbl   for all s in S, c in C
```

Scenario-adjusted sales cap:

```text
sell_sc <= capdem_c * demmult_s                  for all s in S, c in C
```

Contract-floor shortfall definition:

```text
short_sc >= floor_c - sell_sc                    for all s in S, c in C
short_sc >= 0                                    for all s in S, c in C
```

### 6. Tail-risk penalty

Worst priority-contract shortfall:

```text
tail >= sum_{c in C_pri} short_sc                for all s in S
```

## Exported quantities

- Process tons exported to `solution.csv` are `delta * x_sbl`
- `sell_sc`, `h_l`, and all objective calculations remain in ton/hour units

## Public-data assumptions fixed in the implementation

The statement leaves several semantics implicit. The solver fixes them as follows and validates against the exported artifact:

1. **Scenario-adjusted line capacity** uses the labor-drag multiplier:
   - `cap_l / labmult_s`
2. **Must-process commitments** are enforced for every batch with `must_process_rate > 0`, using scenario-specific available tons.
3. **An opened line must process at least `min_open_tons` in every scenario**.
4. **Contract maximum tons** are scenario-adjusted by `demand_multiplier`; **contract floor tons are not scaled**.
5. **Risky processing QC requirement** is `ceil(qcmin_l * contmult_s)` when a batch is mixed or flagged high contamination.
6. **Tail-risk penalty** applies to the worst scenario total shortfall across priority-contract commodities.
7. **Processing residue** equals processed tons minus total recovered clean tons.

# OR PySCIPOpt

## Use when

- The task asks for a Python optimization model using SCIP or `pyscipopt`.
- Existing solver code imports `pyscipopt`, sets SCIP parameters, writes `.lp`/`.mps`, or calls a SCIP-based checker.
- The user asks to run, debug, speed up, validate, or explain a SCIP-based solve.

## Do not use when

- The task only needs a mathematical formulation and no solver-specific code.
- The project clearly uses a different solver stack, such as Pyomo with CBC/HiGHS, OR-Tools CP-SAT, PuLP, CPLEX, or cvxpy, unless the user asks to port it to PySCIPOpt.
- The model is nonlinear or stochastic in a way that SCIP cannot handle directly; first decide whether it can be linearized or approximated.

## Environment rule

Use the Python environment specified by the user or the project. In this ORClaw workspace, the default solver environment is the `or` conda environment with PySCIPOpt available:

```bash
conda run -n or python <script.py>
```

If `pyscipopt` is missing, report the missing dependency, identify the command that failed, and try the project-specified environment before changing solver stacks.

## Workflow

1. Read the task brief and input files before coding.
2. Write the full mathematical model first: sets, parameters, decision variables, objective, and constraints.
3. Implement the model with explicit tuple keys and named constraints.
4. Solve with the selected Python command.
5. Export the solution using the exact schema required by the task.
6. Validate with the task checker or an independent recomputation of feasibility and objective.
7. If the solution is not accepted, diagnose whether the issue is formulation, data parsing, export formatting, tolerance, or evaluator mismatch before changing the objective or constraints.

## PySCIPOpt patterns

- Use `from pyscipopt import Model, quicksum`.
- Create sparse variable dictionaries explicitly, for example:

```python
x = {(i, j, t): model.addVar(vtype="B", name=f"x[{i},{j},{t}]") for i, j, t in keys}
```

- Use `quicksum(...)` for large linear expressions.
- Use `model.addCons(expr <= rhs, name="capacity[k,t]")` for constraints.
- Use `model.setObjective(expr, "minimize")` or `model.setObjective(expr, "maximize")`.
- Set reproducibility and limits with SCIP parameters:

```python
model.setParam("limits/gap", 0.0005)
model.setParam("limits/time", time_limit)
model.setParam("display/verblevel", 0)
```

- After `model.optimize()`, inspect `model.getStatus()` and only read values if a solution exists.
- Read variable values with `model.getVal(x[key])`, not `x[key].X`.
- Read objective and bound with `model.getObjVal()` and `model.getDualbound()`.
- Read relative gap with `model.getGap()` when available.

## Modeling hygiene

- Calibrate every big-M from data. If a tight M is not obvious, derive a bound and document it in a short comment.
- Keep feasibility checks, objective recomputation, and reference-optimality comparison separate.
- Do not round exported decision values unless the schema explicitly requires it.
- For benchmark work, treat the evaluator or checker as the scoring authority and the reference solver as a validation baseline.
- Keep solver logs or a concise solve summary when the user needs auditability.
- Avoid changing data to make a model feasible unless the task is explicitly to design or repair an instance.

## Debugging boundaries

- If a model is infeasible, inspect hard lower bounds, conservation equations, and mutually exclusive binary logic before relaxing constraints.
- If objective values differ, recompute each objective component from exported decisions.
- If runtime is high, first remove dominated variables, tighten big-M values, add eligibility filters, and check whether binaries are really necessary.
- If SCIP returns an incumbent with a gap, say so clearly; do not call it exact optimality unless the status and gap support that claim.
- If an evaluator rejects a solution that the model considers feasible, replay the rejected rule directly from the exported file before changing solver code.

## Useful commands

```bash
python -c "import pyscipopt; print(pyscipopt.__version__)"
conda run -n or python -c "import pyscipopt; print(pyscipopt.__version__)"
<python-command> <solver-script.py>
<python-command> <checker-script.py> --solution <solution-file> --env-dir <task-dir>
```

# Advanced MIP Patterns

## Use when

- The formulation needs logical implications, fixed charges, activation decisions, ordering, no-overlap, disjunctions, min/max, absolute values, tiers, route state, or resource calendars.
- A simple LP/MIP is producing unrealistic behavior because decisions are not linked tightly enough.
- The model is slow or infeasible and the likely cause is weak big-M logic, excessive pairwise variables, or missing state transitions.

## Do not use when

- A continuous LP is sufficient and adding binaries would not represent a real decision.
- The task is better handled by a specialized CP-SAT, constraint programming, nonlinear, or simulation method and the user did not require MIP.
- The user only needs a high-level explanation; do not introduce advanced constructs unless they clarify the actual model.

## General boundary rules

- Add binaries only for real yes/no choices, disjunctions, or nonconvex structure.
- Prefer the tightest formulation that matches the business rule; avoid decorative binaries.
- Use solver-native constructs when they improve clarity and are supported by the target environment.
- If a construct makes the model much slower, compare it with a simpler valid alternative before keeping it.

## Fixed charge and activation

- Use a binary `y` for whether an activity/resource is active.
- Link activity with a tight bound: `x <= U * y`.
- Add lower activation only when the business requires minimum use: `x >= L * y`.
- Charge fixed cost once through `fixed_cost * y`, not per unit.
- Do not force `y = 1` whenever `x = 0` unless the activity is mandatory.
- If there are startup/shutdown costs over time, separate active state from transition state.

## Logical rules

- Prefer solver-native indicator constraints for simple if-then rules when they keep the model clearer.
- If using big-M, derive M from data bounds and keep one M per rule family when possible.
- For either-or choices, use binaries whose sum is exactly one or at most one, matching the business rule.
- Distinguish implication (`A -> B`) from equivalence (`A <-> B`); the reverse direction often needs a separate constraint.
- For conditional capacity, link both activity and resource use to the condition.

## Min, max, absolute value

- For `z = max(a, b)`, add `z >= a`, `z >= b`, and objective pressure if minimizing z. Use binaries only when equality is needed without objective pressure.
- For absolute deviation `d = |x - target|`, add `d >= x - target` and `d >= target - x`.
- For service-level penalties, separate hard feasibility from soft penalty variables.
- For `min`, either transform with signs or add binaries when equality is not enforced by the objective.
- For ratio constraints, cross-multiply only when denominators are constant or safely bounded away from zero.

## Sequencing and no-overlap

- For jobs sharing a resource, use order binary `before[i,j]`.
- Typical pattern:
  - `start[j] >= end[i] - M * (1 - before[i,j])`
  - `start[i] >= end[j] - M * before[i,j]`
- Compute M from the scheduling horizon, not from an arbitrary large number.
- Include setup, cleanup, travel, rest, or turnover time in the correct side of the ordering constraint.
- Create pairwise order variables only for pairs that can share a resource or conflict.
- If time is discrete and the horizon is small, a time-indexed model may be cleaner than pairwise disjunctions.

## Piecewise and tiers

- Use solver-native piecewise-linear features for convex piecewise costs when suitable.
- For nonconvex tiers, use explicit segment variables and segment activation binaries.
- Ensure tier quantities sum to the original activity and each tier has its own upper bound.
- Distinguish incremental tiers from all-units tiers; their formulations and costs differ.
- Check whether discount tiers create nonconvex incentives that require binary selection.

## State and inventory logic

- State variables need initial conditions, transition equations, and terminal requirements if the business specifies them.
- Route or schedule state should be advanced in event order, not checked only row by row.
- Recovery, restock, cleaning, cooldown, maintenance, and rest rules often require cumulative state or last-event logic.
- If state can reset, model the reset trigger and its resource/time cost.

## Debugging advanced models

- First solve a relaxed or smaller instance and inspect whether the intended binaries activate.
- Add named constraints so IIS output points to business rules.
- If the reference solution is infeasible, replay the relevant state transitions manually before changing the evaluator.
- If the LP relaxation is very weak, inspect big-M values, missing lower/upper links, and symmetry.
- If the model is unexpectedly infeasible, test each advanced rule family on a tiny instance with a known feasible solution.

#!/usr/bin/env python3

import csv
import json
import math
import os
import time
from collections import defaultdict

from pyscipopt import Model, quicksum


BASE_DIR = "/app"
DATA_DIR = os.path.join(BASE_DIR, "data")
SUBMISSIONS_DIR = os.path.join(BASE_DIR, "submissions")
MODEL_PATH = os.path.join(SUBMISSIONS_DIR, "model.md")
SOLUTION_PATH = os.path.join(SUBMISSIONS_DIR, "solution.csv")
SOLVE_LOG_PATH = os.path.join(SUBMISSIONS_DIR, "solve_log.md")


def read_csv(path):
    with open(path, newline="", encoding="utf-8") as handle:
        return list(csv.DictReader(handle))


def tons_to_units_exact(tons, increment):
    units = int(round(tons / increment))
    if abs(units * increment - tons) > 1e-8:
        raise ValueError(f"{tons} is not aligned to increment {increment}")
    return units


def ceil_units(tons, increment):
    return int(math.ceil(tons / increment - 1e-9))


def floor_units(tons, increment):
    return int(math.floor(tons / increment + 1e-9))


def fmt(value):
    if abs(value) <= 5e-10:
        value = 0.0
    return f"{value:.8f}"


def build_data():
    lines_raw = read_csv(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "sorting_lines.csv"))
    rec_raw = read_csv(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "line_commodity_recovery.csv"))
    batches_raw = read_csv(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "inbound_batches.csv"))
    comm_raw = read_csv(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "commodities.csv"))
    scenarios_raw = read_csv(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "scenarios.csv"))
    quality_raw = read_csv(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "scenario_batch_quality.csv"))
    with open(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "config.json"), encoding="utf-8") as handle:
        cfg = json.load(handle)

    increment = float(cfg["ton_increment"])
    tolerance = float(cfg["feasibility_tolerance"])

    lines = {}
    line_ids = []
    by_family = defaultdict(list)
    container_lines = []
    for row in lines_raw:
        line_id = row["line_id"]
        line_ids.append(line_id)
        family = row["family"]
        line = {
            "line_id": line_id,
            "family": family,
            "capacity_tons": float(row["capacity_tons"]),
            "min_open_tons": float(row["min_open_tons"]),
            "fixed_cost": float(row["fixed_cost"]),
            "variable_cost_per_ton": float(row["variable_cost_per_ton"]),
            "base_labor_hours": float(row["base_labor_hours"]),
            "labor_hours_per_ton": float(row["labor_hours_per_ton"]),
            "max_labor_hours": float(row["max_labor_hours"]),
            "min_labor_hours": float(row["min_labor_hours"]),
            "min_qc_tests": int(row["min_qc_tests"]),
            "max_qc_tests": int(row["max_qc_tests"]),
            "accepts_streams": set(row["accepts_streams"].split(";")),
            "wet_stream_allowed": int(row["wet_stream_allowed"]),
        }
        lines[line_id] = line
        by_family[family].append(line_id)
        if family in {"metal", "glass_metal", "mixed"}:
            container_lines.append(line_id)

    commodities = {}
    commodity_ids = []
    priority_commodities = []
    for row in comm_raw:
        commodity_id = row["commodity_id"]
        commodity_ids.append(commodity_id)
        commodity = {
            "price_per_ton": float(row["price_per_ton"]),
            "contract_floor_tons": float(row["contract_floor_tons"]),
            "contract_max_tons": float(row["contract_max_tons"]),
            "shortfall_penalty_per_ton": float(row["shortfall_penalty_per_ton"]),
            "priority_contract": int(row["priority_contract"]),
        }
        commodities[commodity_id] = commodity
        if commodity["priority_contract"]:
            priority_commodities.append(commodity_id)

    batches = {}
    batch_ids = []
    for row in batches_raw:
        batch_id = row["batch_id"]
        batch_ids.append(batch_id)
        priority = int(row["municipal_priority"])
        batch = {
            "supplier_zone": row["supplier_zone"],
            "stream": row["stream"],
            "available_tons": float(row["available_tons"]),
            "wet_stream": int(row["wet_stream"]),
            "high_contamination_risk": int(row["high_contamination_risk"]),
            "must_process_rate": float(row["must_process_rate"]),
            "municipal_priority": priority,
            "risky": row["stream"] == "mixed" or int(row["high_contamination_risk"]) == 1,
            "unprocessed_penalty_per_ton": float(
                cfg["unprocessed_priority_penalty_per_ton"]
                if priority
                else cfg["unprocessed_regular_penalty_per_ton"]
            ),
        }
        batches[batch_id] = batch

    scenarios = {}
    scenario_ids = []
    for row in scenarios_raw:
        scenario_id = row["scenario_id"]
        scenario_ids.append(scenario_id)
        scenarios[scenario_id] = {
            "probability": float(row["probability"]),
            "volume_multiplier": float(row["volume_multiplier"]),
            "demand_multiplier": float(row["demand_multiplier"]),
            "contamination_multiplier": float(row["contamination_multiplier"]),
            "labor_multiplier": float(row["labor_multiplier"]),
            "recovery_multiplier": float(row["recovery_multiplier"]),
        }

    quality = {}
    for row in quality_raw:
        scenario_id = row["scenario_id"]
        batch_id = row["batch_id"]
        shares = {
            "OCC": float(row["occ_share"]),
            "MIXPAPER": float(row["mixed_paper_share"]),
            "PET": float(row["pet_share"]),
            "HDPE": float(row["hdpe_share"]),
            "ALUM": float(row["alum_share"]),
        }
        quality[(scenario_id, batch_id)] = {
            "available_tons": float(row["available_tons"]),
            "available_units": tons_to_units_exact(float(row["available_tons"]), increment),
            "shares": shares,
            "residue_share": float(row["residue_share"]),
        }

    recovery = {}
    for row in rec_raw:
        recovery[(row["line_id"], row["commodity_id"])] = float(row["recovery_efficiency"])

    eligible_pairs = []
    eligible_lines_by_batch = defaultdict(list)
    eligible_batches_by_line = defaultdict(list)
    risky_pairs = []
    for batch_id in batch_ids:
        batch = batches[batch_id]
        for line_id in line_ids:
            line = lines[line_id]
            if batch["stream"] not in line["accepts_streams"]:
                continue
            if batch["wet_stream"] and not line["wet_stream_allowed"]:
                continue
            eligible_pairs.append((batch_id, line_id))
            eligible_lines_by_batch[batch_id].append(line_id)
            eligible_batches_by_line[line_id].append(batch_id)
            if batch["risky"]:
                risky_pairs.append((batch_id, line_id))

    capacity_units = {}
    risky_qc_required = {}
    sales_caps = {}
    min_open_units = {}
    for line_id in line_ids:
        min_open_units[line_id] = ceil_units(lines[line_id]["min_open_tons"], increment)

    for scenario_id in scenario_ids:
        scen = scenarios[scenario_id]
        for line_id in line_ids:
            cap_tons = lines[line_id]["capacity_tons"] / scen["labor_multiplier"]
            capacity_units[(scenario_id, line_id)] = floor_units(cap_tons, increment)
            risky_qc_required[(scenario_id, line_id)] = int(
                math.ceil(
                    lines[line_id]["min_qc_tests"] * scen["contamination_multiplier"] - 1e-9
                )
            )
        for commodity_id in commodity_ids:
            sales_caps[(scenario_id, commodity_id)] = (
                commodities[commodity_id]["contract_max_tons"] * scen["demand_multiplier"]
            )

    process_ub = {}
    recovery_coef = {}
    recovered_total_coef = {}
    must_process_units = {}
    for scenario_id in scenario_ids:
        rec_mult = scenarios[scenario_id]["recovery_multiplier"]
        for batch_id in batch_ids:
            avail_units = quality[(scenario_id, batch_id)]["available_units"]
            must_process_units[(scenario_id, batch_id)] = int(
                math.ceil(batches[batch_id]["must_process_rate"] * avail_units - 1e-9)
            )
            shares = quality[(scenario_id, batch_id)]["shares"]
            for line_id in eligible_lines_by_batch[batch_id]:
                ub = min(avail_units, capacity_units[(scenario_id, line_id)])
                process_ub[(scenario_id, batch_id, line_id)] = ub
                total = 0.0
                for commodity_id in commodity_ids:
                    coef = (
                        increment
                        * shares[commodity_id]
                        * rec_mult
                        * recovery[(line_id, commodity_id)]
                    )
                    recovery_coef[(scenario_id, batch_id, line_id, commodity_id)] = coef
                    total += coef
                recovered_total_coef[(scenario_id, batch_id, line_id)] = total

    return {
        "cfg": cfg,
        "increment": increment,
        "tolerance": tolerance,
        "lines": lines,
        "line_ids": line_ids,
        "fiber_lines": list(by_family["fiber"]),
        "plastic_lines": list(by_family["plastic"]),
        "container_lines": container_lines,
        "batches": batches,
        "batch_ids": batch_ids,
        "commodities": commodities,
        "commodity_ids": commodity_ids,
        "priority_commodities": priority_commodities,
        "scenarios": scenarios,
        "scenario_ids": scenario_ids,
        "quality": quality,
        "recovery": recovery,
        "eligible_pairs": eligible_pairs,
        "eligible_lines_by_batch": dict(eligible_lines_by_batch),
        "eligible_batches_by_line": dict(eligible_batches_by_line),
        "risky_pairs": risky_pairs,
        "capacity_units": capacity_units,
        "risky_qc_required": risky_qc_required,
        "sales_caps": sales_caps,
        "process_ub": process_ub,
        "recovery_coef": recovery_coef,
        "recovered_total_coef": recovered_total_coef,
        "must_process_units": must_process_units,
